\title{Robust Gradient Boosting with Soft-Constraint Surrogate Covariance Penalty: Naïve Immediate Adversarial \& Primal-Dual Adversarial}
\author{} % avoid “No \author given” warning
\date{}

\section{General setting and surrogate ``covariance'' idea}

We observe training data $\{(x_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ and fit an additive gradient-boosted model
\begin{equation}
F(x) = F_0(x) + \sum_{t=1}^T \eta_t h_t(x),
\end{equation}
where each $h_t$ is a decision tree (regression) and, for now, we are setting $\eta_t=\eta>0$, a unique learning rate {\color{red}(we can analyze convergence in later implementations with adaptive ones)}. Then, our predictions would be of the form $\yh_i=F(x_i)$.

\paragraph{Base approximation loss.}
For now, we are going to only use MSE, which is the loss that is mainly used by CCAO and is direct to implement, since LightGBM uses the curvature information too. Thus,
\begin{equation}
\ell_i(y, F) \coloneqq (y_i-\yh_i)^2.
\end{equation}
{\color{red} Note: An interesting future work could be using the convergence/bounds guarantees for GSC or similar ones to improve the boosting algorithm for specific. E.g. for GLM losses we have GSC (2nd order bounds and convergence), and the gradients/hessians computations of gradient boosting only need the derivative w.r.t $\hat{y}$, so those properties are enough to set a better algorithm in principle. There is not much about this in the literature other than Frank-Wolfe convergence. Not sure if we can easily extend it to the adversarial setting though. }

\paragraph{Ratio-based surrogate for a covariance-style constraint.} Similar idea of the Adversarial Stable Linear Regression, but now using the triangle inequality's surrogate on the $\ell_2$ norm (same reason as using MSE instead of MAE). That is, given the ratio $r_i(F)=\yh_i/y_i$, we want to, in principle, achieve $r(F) \perp y$. First, we only aim to achieve $\mathrm{Cov}(r(F),y)\approx 0$ as a proxy. Further, to achieve derivative and curvature independence between samples (already used in the linear version and also needed for LGBM's Newton-like descent method), a simple surrogate is achieved using the triangle inequality on the empirical covariance ($\widehat{\text{cov}}$) as
\begin{align*}
    n \cdot |\widehat{\text{cov}}(r(F), y)| =& \left |\sum_{i=1}^n (r_i(F)-\overline{r}(F) )(y_i - \Bar{y}) \right|\\
    \leq & \sqrt{ \sum_{i=1}^n (r_i(F)-\overline{r}(F) )^2(y_i - \Bar{y})^2 }
\end{align*}
Moreover {\color{red} (and this is just for simplicity. Needs a re-thinking)}, we aim to achieve $\Bar{r}(F)\approx 1$, so we use the surrogate proxy-upper bound 
\begin{align}
    ( \widehat{\text{cov}}(r(F), y))^2 \lesssim  \frac{1}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n \phi_i(F) \leq  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \phi_i(F),
\end{align}
where we are using the notation $\lesssim$ since it is not really an upper bound unless the mean of ratios is 1 or another appropriate value, and also
\begin{equation}
\phi_i(F) \coloneqq (r_i(F)-1)^2 (y_i-\bar y)^2
= \Big(\frac{\yh_i}{y_i}-1\Big)^2 (y_i-\bar y)^2,
\qquad \bar y \coloneqq \frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^n y_j.
\label{eq:phisurr}
\end{equation}
\textit{Note: $(y_i-\bar y)^2$ emphasizes ratio errors at extreme $y$ values, as the empirical covariance terms do, while remaining smooth. Also, the term $(r_i(F)-1)^2$ is punishing deviations from the perfect-prediction ratio 1, so is still aiming towards our objective.}
\paragraph{Full-loss: Penalized per-sample objective.} It is a weighted sum of the original MSE loss and the constraint surrogate scoring. We maintain the penalization value $\rho$ as a hyperparameter of the model, to be tuned by the objective set by a decision-maker / stakeholder. The per-sample term of our loss is then 
\begin{equation}
v_i(F) \coloneqq \ell_i(F) + \rho \phi_i(F), \qquad \rho>0.
\label{eq:vi}
\end{equation}

\section{Top-K setting: Adversary weights over samples}

We define an analogous general setting to the one we defined for the linear models. Let $K=\lfloor \texttt{keep}\cdot n\rfloor$ ($0\le \texttt{keep}\le 1$) be a given hyperparameter of the model. As an adversary, we aim to find the worst loss/constraint samples weights over the simplex
\begin{equation}
\Delta_K \coloneqq \{w\in\RR_{+}^n: \one^\top w=1, \quad w_i\le 1/K\ \forall i\},
\end{equation}
for nonnegative vector $v\in\RR^n$. That is, we aim to solve $$\max_{w\in\Delta_K} w^\top v,$$ to get the "worst" mass $1/K$ on the $K$ largest entries of the loss $v$. We aim to achieve through different setting discussed in the next sections.

A first sketch of the general idea is in the pseudo-code of Algorithm \ref{alg:gradient_boosting}. 

\begin{algorithm}[!ht]
\caption{{\color{red}(Sketch of)} Adversarial Gradient Boosting Algorithm}
\label{alg:gradient_boosting}
\begin{algorithmic}[1] % The '[1]' enables line numbering

    % INPUTS
    \Require Training set $\{(x_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^N$, differentiable loss function $L(y, F(x))$, number of iterations $T$, learning rate $\eta$.

    % INITIALIZATION
    \State Initialize model with a constant value:
    $ F_0(x) = \operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\gamma} \sum_{i=1}^N L(y_i, \gamma) $

    % MAIN LOOP
    \For{$t = 1$ to $T$}
        
        \State \textbf{1. Compute pseudo-residuals:}
        \For{$i = 1$ to $N$}
            \State $ r_{it} = - \left[ \frac{\partial L(y_i, F(x_i))}{\partial F(x_i)} \right]_{F(x)=F_{t-1}(x)} \quad \text{ i.e. }(y_i - \hat{y}_{i})^2 \text{ in our case }$
        \EndFor
        \State \textbf{{\color{red}2. Compute some adversarial re-weighting vector $w_t\in \mathbb{R}^n$ (to be defined)}}
        \State \textbf{3. Fit base learner:}
        \State Fit a weak learner (e.g., regression tree) $h_t(x)$ to the re-weighted training data $\{(x_i, r_{it}, w_{it})\}_{i=1}^N$.
        % \State \textbf{3. Compute step size:}
        % \State Solve the 1D optimization problem to find optimal leaf weights:
        % $ \gamma_m = \operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\gamma} \sum_{i=1}^N L(y_i, F_{m-1}(x_i) + \gamma h_m(x_i)) $
        \State \textbf{4. Update model:}
        $ F_t(x) = F_{t-1}(x) + \eta h_t(x) $
        
    \EndFor

    % OUTPUT
    \State \Return Final model $F_T(x)$

\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\section{Naïve Immediate Adversary (Algorithm 1)}

In the Naïve (preliminary) version we aim to implement Gradient-Boosting with the minimal change possible, to mimic the behavior of the linear adversary problems. We also define the two approaches ("adversarial type") that we already defined for the linear models: 

\begin{itemize}
    \item 
\textbf{Overall:} Treat the complete objective function + penalization as the value that the adversary wants to maximize with one variable per sample. In other words, we solve the "approximation"
\texttt{adversary\_type=overall}, the implicit robust objective is
\begin{equation}
\min_F \max_{w\in\Delta_K} \sum_{i=1}^n w_i v_i(F)
\approx \min_F \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i\in \mathrm{TopK}(v(F))} v_i(F),
\label{eq:hardOverall}
\end{equation}
where $\mathrm{TopK}(v)$ denotes any set of indices of the $K$ largest components of $v$.

\item \textbf{Individual:} Treat each component of the objective independently (function loss with one variable and constraint penalization with another one). In other words, we solve the "approximation"
% \subsection{Robust objective viewpoint}
\texttt{adversary\_type=individual}, you instead robustify the two parts separately:
\begin{equation}
\min_F \max_{p\in\Delta_K} \sum_i p_i \ell_i(F)
+ \rho \max_{q\in\Delta_K} \sum_i q_i \phi_i(F).
\label{eq:hardIndividual}
\end{equation}

\end{itemize}



\subsection{Boosting step (gradients/Hessians w.r.t. predictions)}
LightGBM builds each tree using per-sample gradients/Hessians with respect to $\yh_i$.

For $\ell_i(F)=(y_i-\yh_i)^2$:
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial \ell_i}{\partial \yh_i} = 2(\yh_i-y_i),
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2 \ell_i}{\partial \yh_i^2} = 2.
\end{equation}

For $\phi_i$ in \eqref{eq:phisurr} (with $\bar y$ treated as constant):
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial \phi_i}{\partial \yh_i} = 2(\yh_i-y_i)\Big(\frac{y_i-\bar y}{y_i}\Big)^2,
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2 \phi_i}{\partial \yh_i^2} = 2\Big(\frac{y_i-\bar y}{y_i}\Big)^2.
\end{equation}

Algorithm~\ref{alg:hard} uses these derivatives only on the selected worst sets (top-$K$), setting the rest to (near) zero.

\begin{algorithm}[!ht]
\caption{Hard best-response top-$K$ robust boosting (sketch)}
\label{alg:hard}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require Data $\{(x_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$, penalty $\rho>0$, keep fraction giving $K$, rounds $T$.
\State Initialize $F_0$ (e.g., constant mean).
\For{$t=1,2,\dots,T$}
\State $\yh_i \gets F_{t-1}(x_i)$ for all $i$.
\State Compute $\ell_i \gets (y_i-\yh_i)^2$ and $\phi_i \gets (\yh_i/y_i-1)^2(y_i-\bar y)^2$.
\If{overall adversary}
\State $S \gets \mathrm{TopK}(\ell + \rho\phi)$.
\State Set $(g_i,h_i)$ from $\ell+\rho\phi$ for $i\in S$ and $(g_i,h_i)\approx(0,0)$ otherwise.
\Else \Comment{individual adversaries}
\State $S_\ell \gets \mathrm{TopK}(\ell)$ and $S_\phi \gets \mathrm{TopK}(\phi)$.
\State Set $(g_i,h_i)$ from $\ell$ on $S_\ell$ and from $\rho\phi$ on $S_\phi$; add overlaps.
\EndIf
\State Fit tree $h_t$ using $\{(x_i,g_i,h_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ and update $F_t \gets F_{t-1} + \eta h_t$.
\EndFor
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}
\textit{Note:
The adversary is an instantaneous best-response: it selects the current tail (worst $K$) and forces the next tree to focus almost entirely on those points. This can be aggressive and discontinuous when the top-$K$ set changes.
}
\section{Algorithm 2: Primal--dual (smooth) adversary via mirror ascent}

Algorithm~\ref{alg:pd} targets the same saddle structure as \eqref{eq:hardOverall} (or \eqref{eq:hardIndividual}), but replaces the hard best-response by a smooth online update of the adversary weights.

\subsection{Dual update (mirror ascent on $\Delta_K$)}
Maintain weights $w_t\in\Delta_K$ (overall) or $p_t,q_t\in\Delta_K$ (individual). Given per-sample scores, update by exponentiated-gradient (mirror ascent) and project back to $\Delta_K$.

Overall mode uses $v_i(F_t)=\ell_i(F_t)+\rho\phi_i(F_t)$:
\begin{equation}
\tilde w_{t+1,i} = w_{t,i}\exp\big(\eta_{\text{adv}}(v_i(F_t)-\max_{j} v_j(F_t))\big),
\qquad
w_{t+1} = \Pi_{\Delta_K}(\tilde w_{t+1}).
\label{eq:mirror}
\end{equation}
Individual mode applies the same update separately to $\ell(F_t)$ (for $p$) and $\phi(F_t)$ (for $q$).

\subsection{Primal update (weighted derivatives for all samples)}
Instead of masking, the primal step uses weighted gradients/Hessians on \emph{all} samples.

Overall mode:
\begin{equation}
g_i = (n w_{t,i})\Big(\tfrac{\partial \ell_i}{\partial \yh_i} + \rho\tfrac{\partial \phi_i}{\partial \yh_i}\Big),
\qquad
h_i = (n w_{t,i})\Big(\tfrac{\partial^2 \ell_i}{\partial \yh_i^2} + \rho\tfrac{\partial^2 \phi_i}{\partial \yh_i^2}\Big).
\label{eq:weightedGH}
\end{equation}
Individual mode:
\begin{equation}
g_i = (n p_{t,i})\tfrac{\partial \ell_i}{\partial \yh_i} + \rho (n q_{t,i})\tfrac{\partial \phi_i}{\partial \yh_i},
\qquad
h_i = (n p_{t,i})\tfrac{\partial^2 \ell_i}{\partial \yh_i^2} + \rho (n q_{t,i})\tfrac{\partial^2 \phi_i}{\partial \yh_i^2}.
\end{equation}

\begin{algorithm}[t]
\caption{Primal--dual robust boosting with mirror-ascent adversary (sketch)}
\label{alg:pd}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require Data $\{(x_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$, $\rho>0$, keep fraction giving $K$, $\eta_{\text{adv}}>0$, rounds $T$.
\State Initialize $F_0$ and set $w_1=\one/n$ (or $p_1=q_1=\one/n$).
\For{$t=1,2,\dots,T$}
\State $\yh_i \gets F_{t-1}(x_i)$ for all $i$.
\State Compute $\ell_i \gets (y_i-\yh_i)^2$ and $\phi_i \gets (\yh_i/y_i-1)^2(y_i-\bar y)^2$.
\If{overall adversary}
\State $v_i \gets \ell_i + \rho\phi_i$ for all $i$.
\State $w_{t+1} \gets \Pi_{\Delta_K}(w_t\odot \exp(\eta_{\text{adv}} v))$.
\State Set $g_i,h_i$ using \eqref{eq:weightedGH}.
\Else \Comment{individual adversaries}
\State $p_{t+1} \gets \Pi_{\Delta_K}(p_t\odot \exp(\eta_{\text{adv}} \ell))$.
\State $q_{t+1} \gets \Pi_{\Delta_K}(q_t\odot \exp(\eta_{\text{adv}} \phi))$.
\State Set $g_i,h_i$ using the individual weighted formulas.
\EndIf
\State Fit tree $h_t$ using $\{(x_i,g_i,h_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ and update $F_t \gets F_{t-1} + \eta h_t$.
\EndFor
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\paragraph{Intuition.}
Weights act as a soft tail selector: large-score points gradually gain weight, but the cap $w_i\le 1/K$ prevents collapse onto a single sample. Compared to Algorithm~\ref{alg:hard}, the focus shifts smoothly rather than abruptly.

% \section{Practical notes}
% \begin{itemize}
% \item \textbf{Overall vs individual adversaries.} Overall targets the tail of $\ell+\rho\phi$; individual ensures pressure on both tails even when they occur on different samples.
% \item \textbf{Role of $K$ and $\eta_{\text{adv}}$.} Smaller $K$ (smaller \texttt{keep}) and/or larger $\eta_{\text{adv}}$ makes the adversary more concentrated, approaching hard top-$K$ behavior.
% \item \textbf{Division by $y_i$.} In practice one should stabilize $\yh_i/y_i$ when $y_i$ can be near zero (e.g., replace $y_i$ by $\max(|y_i|,\varepsilon)$).
% \end{itemize}

% Section: Penalty, Primal--Dual, and Direct Covariance Objectives
% (to be inserted after the part that already explains LGBMPrimalDual
%  and its predecessors)
% ==============================================================

\section{Three Objective Variants for Regressivity-Aware Boosting}
\label{sec:three_variants}

This section introduces three closely-related variants of the regressivity-aware boosting objectives discussed earlier. The goal is to (i) isolate the effect of the surrogate penalty without any tail-risk reweighting, (ii) provide a principled primal--dual implementation for CVaR/top-$K$ tail control in the LightGBM setting, and (iii) compare the separable surrogate against a more direct (non-separable) covariance objective that is still implementable within LightGBM's custom-gradient interface.

Throughout, we assume we are learning an additive tree ensemble $F$ that outputs predictions $\hat y_i = F(x_i)$ for samples $(x_i,y_i)_{i=1}^n$, with $y_i>0$ (as in price/assessment settings). Let
\begin{align}
\bar y &:= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n y_i, \qquad y_i^c := y_i-\bar y, \\
 r_i(F) &:= \frac{\hat y_i}{y_i} \quad \text{(ratio of predicted to true)}.
\end{align}

\paragraph{LightGBM interface reminder.}
LightGBM fits each new tree by aggregating per-sample first and second derivatives (``gradients'' and ``Hessians'') with respect to the current raw prediction $\hat y_i$ (or the raw score, depending on the objective). When we provide a custom objective, we supply vectors
\begin{align}
 g_i(F) = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \hat y_i}, \qquad h_i(F) = \frac{\partial^2 \mathcal{L}}{\partial \hat y_i^2},
\end{align}
and LightGBM uses these statistics to select splits and compute leaf values (approximately Newton-style, with regularization). This motivates the design of objectives below: we prefer objectives whose derivatives are (at least approximately) representable via per-sample diagonal Hessians.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Variant 1: Plain penalized objective (no dual/adversary)}
\label{subsec:plain_penalty}

The first variant removes the adversary entirely and simply minimizes a penalized risk:
\begin{align}
\min_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\; \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \Big[ (y_i-\hat y_i)^2 + \rho\, s_i(F)\Big],
\end{align}
where $\rho\ge 0$ controls the penalty strength, and $s_i(F)$ is a separable surrogate intended to reduce correlation between the multiplicative error and $y$.

\paragraph{Separable regressivity surrogate.}
We use the same per-sample surrogate as in the previous implementation:
\begin{align}
 s_i(F) 
 := \big(r_i(F)-1\big)^2\,(y_i^c)^2
 = \left(\frac{\hat y_i}{y_i}-1\right)^2 (y_i-\bar y)^2.
\label{eq:surrogate_def}
\end{align}
Intuitively, $s_i$ penalizes multiplicative deviations ($r_i\neq 1$) more strongly for observations far from the mean $\bar y$.

\paragraph{Derivatives (used by LightGBM).}
Let $\hat y_i$ denote the current prediction. Since \eqref{eq:surrogate_def} is separable across $i$, the gradient and Hessian contributions are simple:
\begin{align}
\frac{\partial}{\partial \hat y_i}(y_i-\hat y_i)^2 &= 2(\hat y_i-y_i),
\qquad \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \hat y_i^2}(y_i-\hat y_i)^2 = 2,\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial \hat y_i}s_i(F)
 &= 2(\hat y_i-y_i)\left(\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}\right)^2,
\qquad \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \hat y_i^2}s_i(F)
 = 2\left(\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}\right)^2.
\label{eq:surrogate_derivatives}
\end{align}
Thus, the total (per-sample) derivatives supplied to LightGBM are
\begin{align}
 g_i(F) &= 2(\hat y_i-y_i) + \rho\cdot 2(\hat y_i-y_i)\left(\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}\right)^2, \\
 h_i(F) &= 2 + \rho\cdot 2\left(\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}\right)^2.
\label{eq:plain_penalty_gh}
\end{align}

\paragraph{What this variant tests.}
This baseline answers: \emph{is the surrogate penalty itself enough to improve regressivity-related metrics without any tail-risk control?}
If not, improvements observed in primal--dual versions likely come from the robust reweighting rather than the surrogate itself.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Variant 2: Improved primal--dual CVaR/top-$K$ implementation}
\label{subsec:pd_improved}

The second variant retains the primal--dual viewpoint but uses a more principled dual update consistent with the geometry of probability weights. The robust objective is
\begin{align}
\min_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\; \max_{w\in \Delta_{K}} \sum_{i=1}^n w_i\, v_i(F),
\qquad v_i(F):=(y_i-\hat y_i)^2 + \rho\, s_i(F),
\label{eq:cvar_objective_overall}
\end{align}
where the capped simplex (CVaR dual set) is
\begin{align}
\Delta_K := \Big\{w\in\mathbb{R}^n:\; w\ge 0,\; \sum_{i=1}^n w_i = 1,\; w_i \le \tfrac{1}{K}\ \forall i\Big\},
\qquad K := \lceil \alpha n\rceil,\ \alpha\in(0,1].
\label{eq:capped_simplex}
\end{align}
For $\alpha$ small, the inner maximization concentrates on the worst-tail samples, yielding CVaR/top-$K$ style robustness.

\paragraph{Overall vs. individual adversary.}
As discussed earlier, there are two natural robustifications:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Overall adversary} (single $w$): robustifies the combined per-sample objective $v_i(F)$ as in \eqref{eq:cvar_objective_overall}.
  \item \textbf{Individual adversary} (two weights $p,q$):
  \begin{align}
  \min_F\ \max_{p\in\Delta_K}\sum_i p_i (y_i-\hat y_i)^2\ +\ \rho\,\max_{q\in\Delta_K}\sum_i q_i\, s_i(F).
  \label{eq:cvar_objective_individual}
  \end{align}
  This is typically more conservative because it may emphasize different subsets for MSE and the surrogate.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Primal update (LightGBM-compatible).}
Given current dual weights (either $w$ or $(p,q)$), the primal step is performed implicitly by LightGBM via weighted derivatives. Concretely,
\begin{itemize}
\item In the \textbf{overall} case, we scale per-sample derivatives by $w_i$:
\begin{align}
 g_i \leftarrow w_i\,\big(g_i^{\text{mse}} + \rho g_i^{\text{surr}}\big),\qquad
 h_i \leftarrow w_i\,\big(h_i^{\text{mse}} + \rho h_i^{\text{surr}}\big),
\end{align}
with $(g_i^{\text{mse}},h_i^{\text{mse}})=(2(\hat y_i-y_i),2)$ and $(g_i^{\text{surr}},h_i^{\text{surr}})$ given by \eqref{eq:surrogate_derivatives}.
\item In the \textbf{individual} case, we use $p_i$ for MSE and $q_i$ for the surrogate:
\begin{align}
 g_i \leftarrow p_i\,g_i^{\text{mse}} + \rho\,q_i\,g_i^{\text{surr}},\qquad
 h_i \leftarrow p_i\,h_i^{\text{mse}} + \rho\,q_i\,h_i^{\text{surr}}.
\end{align}
\end{itemize}
(Optionally multiplying weights by $n$ is a scaling convention to keep derivative magnitudes comparable across settings; this interacts with regularization as discussed elsewhere.)

\paragraph{Dual update: (i) exact top-$K$ best response.}
The inner problem $\max_{w\in\Delta_K}\langle w, v\rangle$ has an exact solution:
\begin{align}
 w_i^\star = \begin{cases}
\tfrac{1}{K}, & i\in\text{indices of the largest $K$ entries of }v,\\
0, & \text{otherwise.}
\end{cases}
\label{eq:topk_solution}
\end{align}
This corresponds to ``worst-$K$ averaging'' and is an important ablation: it avoids step-size sensitivity and isolates the effect of robust selection.

\paragraph{Dual update: (ii) exponentiated-gradient (mirror ascent) with KL projection.}
A smooth alternative uses mirror ascent with KL divergence on the simplex:
\begin{align}
 \tilde w_i \propto w_i\,\exp(\eta_{\text{adv}}\, v_i),
\qquad
 w^{+} = \Pi_{\Delta_K}^{\mathrm{KL}}(\tilde w).
\label{eq:mirror_step}
\end{align}
The KL/Bregman projection onto the capped simplex has a closed form:
\begin{align}
 w_i^{+} = \min\Big\{\tfrac{1}{K},\ \tfrac{\tilde w_i}{Z}\Big\},
\quad \text{where $Z>0$ is chosen so that}\quad \sum_{i=1}^n w_i^{+}=1.
\label{eq:kl_projection}
\end{align}
The scalar $Z$ can be found by bisection since the left-hand side is monotone in $Z$. This update is consistent with the geometry of probability weights and avoids the heuristic ``cap-and-redistribute'' projection.

\begin{algorithm}[t]
\caption{Improved primal--dual robust boosting (conceptual)}
\label{alg:pd_improved}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\State Initialize ensemble $F_0$ (LightGBM default: average prediction), choose $\alpha$ (keep fraction) and $K=\lceil\alpha n\rceil$.
\State Initialize dual weights uniformly: $w^0=\tfrac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}$ (or $(p^0,q^0)$).
\For{$t=0,1,\dots,T-1$}
  \State Compute current predictions $\hat y_i = F_t(x_i)$.
  \State Compute per-sample values $v_i(F_t)=(y_i-\hat y_i)^2 + \rho s_i(F_t)$.
  \State \textbf{Dual step:}
    \If{top-$K$}
      \State Set weights by \eqref{eq:topk_solution}.
    \Else
      \State Update by mirror ascent and KL projection \eqref{eq:mirror_step}--\eqref{eq:kl_projection}.
    \EndIf
  \State \textbf{Primal step:} fit next tree with LightGBM using weighted $(g_i,h_i)$ as described above.
\EndFor
\State Return $F_T$.
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\paragraph{What this variant tests.}
This variant addresses: \emph{Do tail-risk/CVaR reweighting and a principled dual update improve out-of-sample robustness of both accuracy and regressivity metrics?} Comparing mirror vs top-$K$ also clarifies whether smooth dual dynamics are needed or if exact best-response is sufficient.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Variant 3: Direct covariance penalty (non-separable, LightGBM-compatible)}
\label{subsec:direct_cov}

The separable surrogate \eqref{eq:surrogate_def} is computationally convenient but only indirectly targets the desired dependence measure between ratio $r$ and $y$. This variant penalizes the (sample) covariance directly:
\begin{align}
\min_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\; \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i-\hat y_i)^2 
\; +\; \rho\, \mathrm{Cov}(r(F),y)^2.
\label{eq:direct_cov_objective}
\end{align}

\paragraph{Simplification of covariance.}
Let $y^c_i=y_i-\bar y$. Since $\frac{1}{n}\sum_i y_i^c=0$, we have
\begin{align}
\mathrm{Cov}(r,y)
= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (r_i-\bar r) y_i^c
= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n r_i y_i^c.
\label{eq:cov_simplification}
\end{align}
Thus the covariance depends on $F$ only through the global scalar
\begin{align}
 c(F) := \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{\hat y_i}{y_i}\, y_i^c.
\end{align}

\paragraph{Derivatives.}
From \eqref{eq:cov_simplification},
\begin{align}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial \hat y_i}
= \frac{1}{n}\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}.
\label{eq:dc_dyhat}
\end{align}
A numerically convenient scaling is to penalize $\tfrac{\rho}{2}n\,c(F)^2$ (so gradients do not vanish as $n$ grows). Define
\begin{align}
\mathcal{P}(F) := \frac{\rho}{2}n\,c(F)^2.
\end{align}
Then the gradient contribution is
\begin{align}
\frac{\partial \mathcal{P}}{\partial \hat y_i}
= \rho n\,c(F)\,\frac{\partial c}{\partial \hat y_i}
= \rho\,c(F)\,\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}.
\label{eq:grad_cov_penalty}
\end{align}

\paragraph{Hessian and the diagonal approximation.}
The exact Hessian of $\mathcal{P}$ is dense (rank-one):
\begin{align}
\nabla^2 \mathcal{P}(F) = \rho n\, \Big(\nabla c(F)\Big)\Big(\nabla c(F)\Big)^\top,
\end{align}
where $\nabla c(F)$ has components $\frac{1}{n}\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}$. LightGBM expects a per-sample (diagonal) Hessian vector; therefore we use a diagonal curvature approximation:
\begin{align}
\frac{\partial^2 \mathcal{P}}{\partial \hat y_i^2}
\approx \rho n\left(\frac{\partial c}{\partial \hat y_i}\right)^2
= \rho\,\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}\right)^2.
\label{eq:hess_diag_cov_penalty}
\end{align}
This approximation preserves the correct scale and monotonicity of curvature but does not capture cross-sample coupling.

\paragraph{Total derivatives for LightGBM.}
Combining MSE and the covariance penalty,
\begin{align}
 g_i(F) &= 2(\hat y_i-y_i) + \rho\,c(F)\,\frac{y_i^c}{y_i},
\\
 h_i(F) &\approx 2 + \rho\,\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}\right)^2.
\label{eq:direct_cov_gh}
\end{align}
The only ``global'' quantity required each iteration is the scalar $c(F)$.

\paragraph{What this variant tests.}
This model directly answers whether performance limitations come from the surrogate (separable proxy) rather than the optimization scheme. If the direct covariance penalty improves the desired dependence measure with competitive accuracy (even under a diagonal Hessian approximation), it suggests the proxy is the bottleneck; if not, it suggests the dependence target itself may be too strict or requires different regularization/robustification.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Discussion and practical consequences}
\label{subsec:discussion_variants}

\paragraph{Proxy vs. true objective.}
The surrogate \eqref{eq:surrogate_def} penalizes $(r_i-1)^2(y_i^c)^2$, which pushes ratios $r_i$ toward 1, especially at extreme $y_i$. The direct covariance penalty instead targets the global alignment of $r$ with $y$ through $c(F)=\frac{1}{n}\sum r_i y_i^c$. These can agree empirically but are not equivalent; Variant~3 provides a clean comparison.

\paragraph{Robustness vs. average performance.}
Variant~2 (CVaR/top-$K$) changes the objective from a mean risk to a tail-focused risk. This can improve worst-case behavior and out-of-sample robustness but may reduce average $R^2$ if $\alpha$ is too large (robustifying too much of the distribution). In practice, small $\alpha$ (e.g., $0.05$--$0.2$) often provides a better robustness/accuracy trade-off than large values.

\paragraph{Numerical stability.}
All ratio-based penalties require care when $y_i$ can be small. In implementation, one typically uses $y_i\leftarrow \max(y_i,\varepsilon)$ to avoid explosive gradients of order $1/y_i^2$.

\paragraph{LightGBM regularization and scaling.}
Because LightGBM uses gradients and Hessians inside split-gain and leaf-value computations, constant rescalings of $(g,h)$ can change the effective strength of regularization parameters such as $\lambda$ and thresholds such as \texttt{min\_sum\_hessian\_in\_leaf}. This explains why custom objectives can generalize differently than the built-in MSE objective even when $\rho=0$.

\paragraph{Recommended experimental comparisons.}
To disentangle modeling from optimization effects, we recommend:
(i) Variant~1 vs. standard MSE (tests surrogate-only impact),
(ii) Variant~2 mirror vs. top-$K$ (tests dual smoothness vs. exact best response), and
(iii) Variant~1 vs. Variant~3 (tests proxy vs. direct dependence objective).


\newpage 

\section{Two LightGBM Custom Objectives Approaches for Regressivity: Direct (Approximate) Covariance Penalty vs.\ Separable Surrogate}
\label{sec:cov_vs_surrogate_lgbm}

Let $\{(x_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ be the training data with $y_i\in\mathbb{R}$ (e.g., house prices). Let
\[
\hat y_i \;\triangleq\; F(x_i)
\]
denote the current model prediction (LightGBM ensemble score) at $x_i$. Define the \emph{ratio}
\[
r_i \;\triangleq\; \frac{\hat y_i}{y_i},
\qquad
\bar y \triangleq \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n y_i,
\qquad
y_i^c \triangleq y_i-\bar y.
\]
% In code, division is stabilized using $\mathrm{denom}_i=\max(|y_i|,\varepsilon_y)$ so that $r_i=\hat y_i/\mathrm{denom}_i$.

Both objectives below start from a \emph{base accuracy loss} (MSE) and add a penalty intended to reduce dependence between the ratio $r$ and the level $y$ ($R \perp Y $ as a proxy for regressivity):
\[
\text{Base MSE:}\quad \ell_i^{\mathrm{mse}}(\hat y_i) \;=\; (y_i-\hat y_i)^2.
\]

\subsection{How LightGBM Works with Custom Objectives}
\label{subsec:lgbm_custom_obj}

LightGBM fits trees by repeatedly minimizing a second-order (Newton/Taylor) approximation of the global objective with respect to additive function updates (boosting predicting an approximation of the residuals). Given a per-sample loss $\ell_i(\hat y_i)$, LightGBM expects the custom objective to return, for each sample $i$,
\[
g_i \;\triangleq\; \frac{\partial \ell_i}{\partial \hat y_i},
\qquad
h_i \;\triangleq\; \frac{\partial^2 \ell_i}{\partial \hat y_i^2},
\]
(or their scaled variants) {\color{red} as vectors (diagonal matrix for hess.)}, which it uses to:
\begin{itemize}
  \item compute split gains, and
  \item compute optimal leaf values via the standard closed form (2nd order)
  \[
  \gamma^* \;=\; -\frac{\sum_{i\in \text{leaf}} g_i}{\sum_{i\in \text{leaf}} h_i + \lambda_{\mathrm{L2}}}
  \quad
  (\text{\color{red} up to LightGBM's internal regularization, which I haven't checked in code}).
  \]
\end{itemize}
A key constraint: LightGBM is designed around \emph{diagonal} per-sample curvature (it wants a vector $(h_i)$), while some global penalties {\color{red} (exact covariance)} induce \emph{dense} Hessians. This is the central difference between the two classes below.

\subsection{Class \texttt{LGBCovPenalty}: Direct Covariance Penalty (Non-separable)}
\label{subsec:lgb_cov_penalty}

\paragraph{Penalty definition.}
The class \texttt{LGBCovPenalty} penalizes the \emph{sample covariance} between $r$ and $y$:
\[
\mathrm{Cov}(r,y)
\;\triangleq\;
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n r_i(y_i-\bar y)
\;=\;
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n r_i\,y_i^c.
\]
Because $\frac{1}{n}\sum_i y_i^c = 0$, this equals $\frac{1}{n}\sum_i r_i y_i^c$ without needing to center $r$.

\paragraph{Objective (with scaling).}
The implemented objective is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cov_obj}
J(\hat y)
\;=\;
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i-\hat y_i)^2
\;+\;
\frac{1}{2}\,\rho\,n\;\Big(\mathrm{Cov}(r,y)\Big)^2.
\end{equation}
{\color{red} NOTE: The factor $\frac{1}{2}\rho n$ is just a \emph{normalization choice}: without $n$, the covariance term scales like $O(1)$ but its gradient can scale like $O(1/n)$; multiplying by $n$ keeps gradient magnitudes comparable across dataset sizes. [NOT 100\% SURE]}

\paragraph{Derivative of the covariance term.}
Write $\mathrm{denom}_i=\max(|y_i|,\varepsilon_y)$ and $r_i=\hat y_i/\mathrm{denom}_i$. Then
\[
c \;\triangleq\; \mathrm{Cov}(r,y) \;=\; \frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^n \frac{\hat y_j}{\mathrm{denom}_j}\,y_j^c.
\]
Hence
\[
\frac{\partial c}{\partial \hat y_i}
\;=\;
\frac{1}{n}\,\frac{y_i^c}{\mathrm{denom}_i}
\;\triangleq\;
a_i.
\]
(The code uses exactly $a_i = (y_i^c/\mathrm{denom}_i)/n$.)

\paragraph{Gradient.}
Let $p(\hat y)=\frac{1}{2}\rho n c^2$. By the chain rule,
\[
\frac{\partial p}{\partial \hat y_i}
\;=\;
\frac{1}{2}\rho n\cdot 2c\cdot \frac{\partial c}{\partial \hat y_i}
\;=\;
\rho n\,c\,a_i.
\]
The base MSE gradient is $\partial (y_i-\hat y_i)^2/\partial \hat y_i = 2(\hat y_i-y_i)$, so the total gradient returned to LightGBM is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cov_grad}
g_i
\;=\;
2(\hat y_i-y_i)
\;+\;
\rho n\,c\,a_i.
\end{equation}
This matches the implementation:
\[
\texttt{grad\_base}=2(\hat y-y),\quad
\texttt{grad\_pen}=\rho n\,c\,a.
\]

\paragraph{True Hessian is probably dense during iterations.}
Since $c$ is linear in $\hat y$, $\frac{\partial^2 c}{\partial \hat y_i \partial \hat y_j}=0$. Thus
\[
\nabla^2 p(\hat y)
\;=\;
\rho n \,(\nabla c)(\nabla c)^\top
\;=\;
\rho n \, a a^\top,
\]
which is dense but rank-one. The true per-sample second derivative (diagonal) is
\[
\frac{\partial^2 p}{\partial \hat y_i^2}
=
\rho n\,a_i^2.
\]

% \paragraph{Diagonal Hessian approximation for LightGBM.}

{\color{red} NOTE: LightGBM requires a vector $(h_i)$, not a dense matrix. \texttt{LGBCovPenalty} uses the diagonal of the true Hessian.}
% \begin{equation}
% \label{eq:cov_hess_diag}
% h_i
% \;\approx\;
% 2
% \;+\;
% \rho n\,a_i^2,
% \end{equation}
% where $2$ comes from the MSE Hessian. This is exactly what the code returns as
% \[
% \texttt{hess\_base}=2,\quad \texttt{hess\_pen}=\rho n a^2.
% \]
% Interpretation: you are optimizing a global penalty, but fitting each Newton step using only \emph{per-sample curvature}. This is the standard compromise when injecting global statistics into tree boosters.

\paragraph{Algorithmic meaning.}
At boosting iteration $t$, with current predictions $\hat y^{(t)}$, the custom objective computes:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $r^{(t)}$ and $c^{(t)}=\mathrm{Cov}(r^{(t)},y)$,
  \item per-sample gradients $g_i^{(t)}$ via \eqref{eq:cov_grad},
  \item per-sample Hessians $h_i^{(t)}$ via \eqref{eq:cov_hess_diag},
\end{enumerate}
and LightGBM fits a tree that approximately minimizes the second-order local model
\[
\sum_{i=1}^n \Big(g_i^{(t)}\,\delta_i + \tfrac{1}{2}h_i^{(t)}\,\delta_i^2\Big)
\]
over tree-structured updates $\delta_i = h_t(x_i)$.

\paragraph{What this penalty is really enforcing.}
Because $c=\frac{1}{n}\sum_i r_i y_i^c$, reducing $c^2$ pushes the weighted average of ratios $r_i$ to be \emph{uncorrelated with} (centered) $y_i$. In regressivity terms: it discourages systematic over/under-prediction that increases with price level.

\subsection{Class \texttt{LGBSmoothPenalty}: Separable Surrogate (Fully diagonal Hessian)}
\label{subsec:lgb_smooth_penalty}

\paragraph{Surrogate definition.}
This class replaces the global covariance penalty with a \emph{per-sample separable} surrogate:
\[
s_i(\hat y_i)
\;\triangleq\;
\Big(\frac{\hat y_i}{y_i}-1\Big)^2\,(y_i-\bar y)^2
=
(\hat y_i-y_i)^2\Big(\frac{y_i^c}{y_i}\Big)^2,
\]
again implemented with $\mathrm{denom}_i$ for stability.

\paragraph{Objective.}
The objective is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:surr_obj}
J_{\mathrm{smooth}}(\hat y)
\;=\;
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i-\hat y_i)^2
\;+\;
\rho\cdot \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n s_i(\hat y_i).
\end{equation}
Unlike \eqref{eq:cov_obj}, the penalty is a sum of independent per-sample terms, so both gradient and Hessian are naturally diagonal.

\paragraph{Gradient and Hessian (exact).}
Define the per-sample scale
\[
\mathrm{scale}_i \;\triangleq\; \Big(\frac{y_i^c}{\mathrm{denom}_i}\Big)^2.
\]
Then
\[
s_i(\hat y_i)
=
(\hat y_i-y_i)^2\,\mathrm{scale}_i,
\quad
\frac{\partial s_i}{\partial \hat y_i}
=
2(\hat y_i-y_i)\,\mathrm{scale}_i,
\quad
\frac{\partial^2 s_i}{\partial \hat y_i^2}
=
2\,\mathrm{scale}_i.
\]
Therefore the custom objective returns
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:surr_grad_hess}
g_i
=
2(\hat y_i-y_i) + \rho\cdot 2(\hat y_i-y_i)\,\mathrm{scale}_i,
\qquad
h_i
=
2 + \rho\cdot 2\,\mathrm{scale}_i,
\end{equation}
which is exactly the code:
\[
\texttt{grad\_pen}=2(\hat y-y)\,\texttt{scale},\quad
\texttt{hess\_pen}=2\,\texttt{scale}.
\]

\paragraph{What this surrogate is encouraging.}
This penalty shrinks $(\hat y_i-y_i)^2$ \emph{more strongly} when $|y_i^c|$ is large relative to $|y_i|$. Intuitively:
\begin{itemize}
  \item high-price and low-price extremes (large $|y_i-\bar y|$) are penalized more,
  \item the penalty is normalized by $y_i^2$ (through the ratio), so it targets \emph{relative} error patterns.
\end{itemize}
It does \emph{not} equal $\mathrm{Cov}(r,y)^2$, but it is a tractable separable proxy that often reduces dependence of ratio errors on the price level in practice.

\subsection{Comparison, Modeling Tradeoffs, and Practical Notes}
\label{subsec:comparison_notes}

\paragraph{Global vs.\ separable.}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \texttt{LGBCovPenalty} directly penalizes a \emph{global} statistic $c=\mathrm{Cov}(r,y)$, which is conceptually clean but induces a dense Hessian. You approximate curvature by its diagonal.
  \item \texttt{LGBSmoothPenalty} is fully separable and matches LightGBM's assumptions exactly (diagonal Hessian). It is easier to optimize reliably, but the penalty is an approximation to the fairness target.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Why the diagonal approximation is reasonable (and when it is not).}
Since the covariance Hessian is rank-one, the diagonal captures the per-coordinate curvature but ignores the coupling that enforces a coherent global adjustment. This typically works as a heuristic, but it can:
\begin{itemize}
  \item under-estimate coupling effects (slower or biased progress on $c$),
  \item become sensitive to normalization (choice of $n$-scaling and $\varepsilon_y$),
  \item interact with LightGBM regularization/hyperparameters (e.g., \texttt{min\_sum\_hessian\_in\_leaf}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Numerical stabilizers used in both classes.}
\begin{itemize}
  \item $\mathrm{denom}_i=\max(|y_i|,\varepsilon_y)$ prevents exploding ratios.
  \item \texttt{zero\_grad\_tol} floors very small gradients/Hessians to keep LightGBM stable.
\end{itemize}

% \paragraph{Interpretation for regressivity.}
% If $r_i=\hat y_i/y_i$ tends to increase with $y_i$, then higher-priced homes are systematically over-assessed relative to lower-priced homes (or vice versa). Penalizing $\mathrm{Cov}(r,y)^2$ (directly or indirectly) targets exactly this systematic dependence.

\section{Understanding Behavior of Results}

We are moving in two different spaces; (1) the normal random variable (conditional on the data matrix $X$) $Z|X \in N(\mu(X), \sigma^2(X))$, and (2) the log-normal random variable $Y = e^Z$. One space is where the real prices $y$ of the  house on the market move (log-normal), and the other one is just aiming to create a symmetric normal distribution $\log(y)=z$. The regression problem on each one of theses cases differs in some aspects. Lets consider 
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Regression Problem in $Y$} (log-normal). We aim to solve $g^* \in \arg\min_g \Expected{(g(X)-Y)^2}$. 
    \item 
\textbf{Regression Problem in $Z$} (normal). We aim to solve $f^* \in \arg\min_f \Expected{(f(X)-Z)^2}$.
\end{itemize}
% \textbf{Regression Problem in $Y$} (log-normal). We aim to solve $f^* \in \arg\min_f \Expected{(f(X)-Y)^2}$.
 That is, in both cases we aim to minimize the mean squared error (MSE). Note that both solutions regress to the mean of their respective r.v., i.e. $g^*(X)=\Expected{Y | X}$ and $f^*(X)=\Expected{Z | X}$, but this does not imply they are equivalent when predicting $Y$. 

Lets first mention that the median, mean, and variance of the Log-Normal distribution are $median(Y|X)=\mu(X)$, $\Expected{Y|X}=\exp{(\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2(X)}{2})}$, and $Var(Y|X)=\Expected{Y|X}^2(\exp{(\sigma^2(X))-1})$. From the mean, we can note that $$g^*(X)=\Expected{Y|X}=\exp{(\Expected{Z|X})} \exp{(\frac{\sigma^2(X)}{2})}=\exp{(f^*(X))} \exp{(\frac{\sigma^2(X)}{2})}.$$
Now, lets inspect the residuals of each model (in the exponential scale) as
\begin{align*}
    \Expected{Y - g^*(X)|X} =& \Expected{Y|X} - g^*(X) = g^*(X)- g^*(X) = 0, \text{ and}\\
    \Expected{Y - e^{f^*(X)}|X} =& \Expected{Y|X} - g^*(X)e^{-\sigma^2/2} = g^*(X)- g^*(X)e^{-\sigma^2(X)/2},
\end{align*}
which is only zero if $\sigma^2(X)=0$ or if $g^*(X)=0$. In other words, \textbf{$\exp{(f^*(X))}$ is a biased estimator of $Y$}. Further, from Jensen's inequality, we get that is it \textbf{underestimating $Y$}, since $\exp$ is a convex function and thus $$g^*(X)= \Expected{\exp{(Z)} | X}\geq \exp{(\Expected{Z | X})} = \exp{(f^*(X))}.$$ 
{\color{red} Note: This could also have been inferred directly from the mean of normal v/s log-normal, but its ok.}
 
 That is a key difference between solving in the log-normal versus solving in normal space and then apply exponentiation over the predictions. We even have an explicit form for $g^*(X)$ in terms of $X$ and the normal distribution's parameters (see Log-Normal distribution)
 $$g^*(X) = \Expected{Y|X} = \exp{\left(\mu(X) + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2(X)\right)},$$
and so we expect that the samples with higher variance will have greater error when solving on $g$. 

\noindent \textbf{The deviation term.} 
We are interested in studying the deviation of our prediction from the real target $Y$, given the data r.v. $X$. Let us define $D_g=g^*(X)-Y$, then $$\Expected{D_g|X}=\Expected{g^*(X)|X}- \Expected{Y|X} = g^*(X)-g^*(X)=0$$
\begin{align*}
    Var(D_g) &= \Expected{D^2|X} - \Expected{D|X}^2 \\&=  \Expected{(g^* - Y)^2|X}\\&= Var(Y|X)\\ &=(\exp{(\sigma^2(X))} - 1 )\exp{\left(2 \mu(X) + \sigma^2(X)\right)}\\ &= (\exp{(\sigma^2(X))} - 1 )\exp{\left(\mu(X) + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2(X)\right)}^2\\&=(\exp{(\sigma^2(X)) -1 )\Expected{Y|X}^2 }
    \\&=(\exp{(\sigma^2(X)) -1 )g^*(X)^2 },
\end{align*} 
and thus, the higher the variance of $g^*(X)$ or the higher the variance of $Y$ (low-values / high-values), the more variance we would have in this term with this approach.

\noindent {\color{red} NOTE: Thus, it does not make much sense to measure R2 on the real price scale, since it is: (1) over-rewarding large values of houses (high $g^*(X)$), and (2) }


Now, lets compute for the log-price, i.e $$ Var(\exp{(Z)} - Y) = \ldots Var(Y|X)$$ (but is often lower because of the log-scale (?).

The mean is not zero tho, it is $E(D_f|X)=E(\exp(Z)|X)- E(Y|X)=\exp(f^*(X)) - g^*(X) = \ldots$
It is biased, but the experiments give less variance at least. And, with the covariance constraint, it improves significantly.

\newpage


\section{Derivation of First-Order Bias for Penalized Log-Normal Regression}

\subsection{Preliminaries and Definitions}

Let $Y$ be a random variable representing the price, and $X$ be the associated feature vector. We assume a conditional Log-Normal distribution for the target variable:
\begin{equation}
    Y | X = \exp(Z), \quad \text{where } Z | X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu(X), \sigma^2)
\end{equation}
Here, $\mu(X)$ is the true conditional mean of the log-prices, and $\sigma^2$ is the constant variance of the log-prices (homoscedasticity assumption).

We define the standard moments of $Y$ conditioned on $X$:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[Y | X] &= \exp\left(\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}\right) \\
    \mathbb{E}[Y^{-1} | X] &= \mathbb{E}[\exp(-Z) | X] = \exp\left(-\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}\right)
\end{align}
Let $\bar{Y} = \mathbb{E}[Y]$ denote the global mean of the target variable.

We seek the bias of an estimator $\hat{Y}(X) = \exp(f^*(X))$, where $f^*(X)$ is the optimal log-prediction derived from a penalized loss function. The bias is defined as:
\begin{equation}
    \text{Bias}(X) = \mathbb{E}[\hat{Y}(X) - Y | X] = \exp(f^*(X)) - \exp\left(\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}\right)
\end{equation}

\subsection{Case 1: Penalization on Covariance (Regressivity Correction)}

We define the optimization objective as minimizing the Mean Squared Error (MSE) on the log-scale, subject to a penalty that maximizes the covariance between the prediction ratio $R$ and the target $Y$ (to correct negative correlation/regressivity).

The loss function $\mathcal{L}$ for the log-prediction $f(X)$ is:
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{L}(f) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(f(X) - Z)^2 | X] - \rho \cdot \text{Cov}(R, Y | X)
\end{equation}
where $R = \frac{\exp(f(X))}{Y}$ and $\rho > 0$ is the penalty weight.

\subsubsection{Gradient Derivation}
First, we expand the covariance term conditioned on $X$. Note that conditioned on $X$, $f(X)$ is deterministic.
\begin{align}
    \text{Cov}(R, Y | X) &= \mathbb{E}[R \cdot Y | X] - \mathbb{E}[R | X]\mathbb{E}[Y | X] \notag \\
    &= \mathbb{E}\left[ \frac{e^{f(X)}}{Y} \cdot Y \bigg| X \right] - \mathbb{E}\left[ \frac{e^{f(X)}}{Y} \bigg| X \right] \bar{Y} \notag \\
    &= e^{f(X)} - e^{f(X)}\mathbb{E}[Y^{-1} | X] \bar{Y}
\end{align}
\textit{Note: We use the global mean $\bar{Y}$ in the covariance expansion as the penalty operates over the global joint distribution, but we analyze the gradient pointwise for $X$.}

The stationarity condition requires $\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial f} = 0$:
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial}{\partial f} \left( \frac{1}{2}(f - \mu)^2 - \rho e^f (1 - \bar{Y}\mathbb{E}[Y^{-1}|X]) \right) &= 0 \notag \\
    (f^*(X) - \mu(X)) - \rho e^{f^*(X)} \left( 1 - \bar{Y} e^{-\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}} \right) &= 0
\end{align}
This yields the implicit equilibrium equation:
\begin{equation} \label{eq:implicit}
    f^*(X) - \mu(X) = \rho e^{f^*(X)} \left( 1 - \bar{Y} e^{-\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}} \right)
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{First-Order Approximation of the Solution}
We assume $\rho$ is small, implying $f^*(X) \approx \mu(X)$. Let $f^*(X) = \mu(X) + \Delta$, where $\Delta$ is a small perturbation.
Substituting $e^{f^*} \approx e^{\mu}$ (zeroth-order approximation for the exponential term) into Eq. \eqref{eq:implicit}:
\begin{equation}
    \Delta \approx \rho e^{\mu(X)} \left( 1 - \bar{Y} e^{-\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}} \right)
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Bias Calculation}
Using the first-order Taylor expansion $\exp(f^*) \approx e^{\mu}(1 + \Delta)$:
\begin{align}
    \text{Bias}(X) &= \exp(f^*) - \mathbb{E}[Y|X] \notag \\
    &\approx e^{\mu(X)}(1 + \Delta) - e^{\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}} \notag \\
    &= \underbrace{e^{\mu(X)}(1 - e^{\frac{\sigma^2}{2}})}_{\text{Standard Log-Normal Bias}} + e^{\mu(X)}\Delta
\end{align}
Substituting $\Delta$:
\begin{equation}
    \text{Bias}_{\text{Cov}}(X) \approx e^{\mu(X)}\left(1 - e^{\frac{\sigma^2}{2}}\right) + \rho e^{2\mu(X)} \left( 1 - \bar{Y} e^{-\mu(X) + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}} \right)
\end{equation}

\subsection{Case 2: Penalization via Separable Surrogate}

Here we consider a loss function using a surrogate for the independence of $R$ and $Y$, defined by the absolute value of the product of deviations:
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{L}_{\text{surr}}(f) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(f(X) - Z)^2] + \rho \mathbb{E}[ | (R - \bar{R})(Y - \bar{Y}) | ]
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Equivalence Proof via Monotonicity}
Under the Log-Normal assumption, there is a strict inverse monotonicity between $Y$ and $R$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $Y$ is large ($Y > \bar{Y}$), then $R = e^f/Y$ is small ($R < \bar{R}$). Thus, $(R - \bar{R}) < 0$ and $(Y - \bar{Y}) > 0$. The product is negative.
    \item If $Y$ is small ($Y < \bar{Y}$), then $R$ is large ($R > \bar{R}$). Thus, $(R - \bar{R}) > 0$ and $(Y - \bar{Y}) < 0$. The product is negative.
\end{itemize}
Consequently, the term inside the absolute value is strictly negative almost everywhere:
\begin{equation}
    | (R - \bar{R})(Y - \bar{Y}) | = - (R - \bar{R})(Y - \bar{Y})
\end{equation}
Taking the expectation:
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}[ - (R - \bar{R})(Y - \bar{Y}) ] = - \text{Cov}(R, Y)
\end{equation}
Thus, minimizing the surrogate $\mathcal{L}_{\text{surr}}$ is analytically equivalent to minimizing the negative covariance (maximizing covariance) as in Case 1.

\subsubsection{Resulting Bias}
Since the objective functions are identical (up to the interpretation of the empirical sample expectation vs. analytical expectation), the first-order gradient dynamics and resulting bias are identical.

\begin{equation}
    \text{Bias}_{\text{Surr}}(X) \approx \underbrace{e^{\mu(X)}\left(1 - e^{\frac{\sigma^2}{2}}\right)}_{\text{Structural Underestimation}} + \underbrace{\rho e^{2\mu(X)} \left( 1 - \frac{\bar{Y}}{e^{\mu(X) - \frac{\sigma^2}{2}}} \right)}_{\text{Regressivity Correction}}
\end{equation}

This confirms that the surrogate introduces a **quadratic correction in price space**: it adds a positive bias that scales with the square of the predicted price for high-value items, effectively counteracting the inherent regressivity of the log-linear model.
